## [assistant] (14:58:45)

**Recommendation: `launch.json`** — it's strictly better here.

**Why launch.json wins over raw terminals:**
- One click (or F5) starts any service — no remembering mvn flags or profile names
- VS Code shows each process in its own named panel in the Debug Console / Terminal
- You can define a **compound** that starts all three (admin + reader + UI) in one action
- Attach a debugger to the Spring Boot processes without any extra steps

**What to set up — three configs + one compound:**

| Name | Command |
|---|---|
| `API: Reader (8080)` | `./dev.sh reader` (or inline mvn) |
| `API: Admin (8081)` | `./dev.sh admin` |
| `UI: Vite dev` | `npm run dev` in the UI folder |
| **`Dev: Full Stack`** | compound — launches all three |

Since `dev.sh` is a bash script (uses `bash` tool), VS Code can run it as a shell task inside a launch config via `"type": "shell"` in `tasks.json`, and the launch configs just invoke those tasks. Or we can inline the mvn commands directly in `launch.json` using the `"type": "java"` launcher (no shell needed) — that also gives proper Spring Boot debug attach.

**Which approach do you want?**
- **Java launcher** — native Spring Boot debug, slightly more config, works without WSL/Git Bash
- **Shell task** — just wraps `./dev.sh`, simpler, reuses what you have, requires bash on PATH

## [assistant] (16:21:01)

This document is from an AI (looks like ChatGPT) exploring a BERT + NLLB neural machine translation approach. Let me give you my honest read on whether it applies to your problem.

---

**The document is solving the wrong problem.**

The awkward 1 Corinthians 6:1-5 output is not caused by corrupted text or lacunae. MorphGNT is a clean, scholarly digital text — every token has a surface form and morphology code. BERT's text reconstruction module is designed for physically damaged manuscripts with missing words. That is not your situation.

**What is actually causing the awkward output:**

Looking at the passage specifically:
- ἐπί → "upon" — should be "before" (legal sense: "before a court of")
- κρίνεσθαι → "to judge" — it is middle/passive: "to go to law / be judged"
- ὑμῶν → "your" — should be "of you" (genitive partitive)
- Articles like τῶν → "the/this/who" is technically the full gloss but reads terribly in context

These are **gloss quality problems**, not missing text problems.

**On the all-AI NLLB route:**

NLLB produces fluent, natural sentences — but it is not an interlinear. It breaks the word-for-word correspondence that is the entire purpose of your app. You would end up with a translation app, not an interlinear. The BSB translation column you already have does what NLLB would do.

**What actually needs to improve:**

1. **Context-sensitive gloss selection for prepositions** — ἐπί means "upon," "on," "before," "against" depending on case and context. The rules engine needs to factor in the following noun's case.
2. **Deponent and middle/passive voice glosses** — κρίνομαι is not "judge" but "go to law / be judged."
3. **Article suppression** — τῶν before a substantive adjective ("the unrighteous ones") doesn't need its own gloss token shown.

Your current architecture — deterministic DB + rules engine — is **correct for an interlinear**. The pipeline is not the problem. The gloss rules need refinement for specific grammatical cases. That is within the existing `InflectionEngineService` and `cluster_gloss_rule` design, not a reason to replace it.

---

Want me to open an issue for improving preposition context-sensitivity in the rules engine, since that appears to be the biggest single contributor to the awkward reading?

## [user] (16:29:49)

what Ai is irrelevant? I seprated it so you are not confused? Critic of your design, in Issues In Current Design.md

## [assistant] (16:30:47)

My apologies for dismissing it. Let me engage with the specific critiques properly.

The document raises **4 criticisms of the current design**. Here is my honest assessment of each:

---

**Critique 1 — No lacunae support in `verse_word`**

Partially already handled. MorphGNT has gap lemmas (tokens with no lexeme match), and the design has a gap lemma mechanism. But the critique is right that the schema doesn't formally model "unknown/uncertain" tokens. Low priority for MorphGNT-based text, but worth noting.

**Critique 2 — GlossChainService can't accommodate probabilistic/context-aware predictions**

This is the most valid critique and directly explains the 1 Cor 6:1-5 problem. The current chain is:

```
user override → gloss_set cache → cluster_gloss_rule → corpus/english_gloss → surface_form
```

Every layer is deterministic. ἐπί always resolves to "upon" regardless of whether the following noun is a courtroom or a hill. There is no layer that can say "in this legal context, ἐπί means *before*." An AI-assisted or context-sensitive layer between `cluster_gloss_rule` and the fallbacks is a real gap.

**Critique 3 — Linear build-time pipeline can't use context**

Also valid. Handler D resolves glosses word-by-word at import time with no awareness of surrounding words. A preposition's gloss is baked in at build time without knowing what follows it in the verse.

**Critique 4 — API contract exposes no confidence or reconstruction metadata**

Valid future concern but not the current bottleneck.

---

**Where the document's proposed fix goes wrong:**

BERT text reconstruction and NLLB full translation solve these problems by destroying the interlinear. NLLB produces one fluent English sentence — you lose word-for-word correspondence entirely, which is the app's purpose. That is not the right direction.

**What the critiques actually point toward:**

A context-aware gloss selection layer — not replacing words, but choosing *which gloss* to use for a given word based on its grammatical neighbors. This stays within the interlinear model but makes it dramatically more readable.

Do you want to discuss what that layer would look like architecturally?

## [assistant] (16:45:23)

You're right. Let me be direct about the actual design flaw.

**The core problem:** Every gloss is computed in isolation. Each Greek word gets an English word picked from a dictionary, with no awareness of the words around it. Greek doesn't work that way and neither does any NLP system that produces readable output.

---

**What Google Translate actually does differently:**

It sees the full clause before deciding what any individual word means. ἐπί doesn't get mapped to "upon" — it gets mapped to "before" because the model sees it sitting between a legal verb (κρίνεσθαι) and a genitive noun phrase. The context determines the gloss.

**Your system does the opposite:** bakes the gloss in at import time, word-by-word, with zero clause context.

---

**A concrete solution that keeps word-for-word:**

Add a **contextual gloss layer** — a new column `contextual_gloss` on `verse_word` (or a separate table), populated at import time using Groq.

The prompt is simple: give Groq the full Greek verse and ask for the best single English word or short phrase for each token in context:

```
Verse: "Τολμᾷ τις ὑμῶν πρᾶγμα ἔχων πρὸς τὸν ἕτερον κρίνεσθαι ἐπὶ τῶν ἀδίκων..."
Word 8: ἐπὶ (preposition, genitive)
What is the best English rendering of this specific word in this specific clause context? 
Reply with one word or short phrase only.
→ "before"
```

This gives you:
- Word-for-word correspondence preserved
- Each gloss aware of its clause context
- No GPU, no NLLB, no architectural rebuild
- Groq free tier already integrated

**In the GlossChainService** it becomes Layer 2.5 — sits between user overrides and the current rule-based chain:

```
user_token_gloss
user_lexeme_gloss
→ contextual_gloss (new — AI, clause-aware, computed once at import)
→ gloss_set_entry (current — rule-based, context-blind)
→ cluster_gloss_rule
→ fallbacks
```

---

**What this does NOT require:**
- Replacing MorphGNT (still use it for morphology)
- BERT or NLLB
- GPU infrastructure
- Abandoning the interlinear model

**What it does require:**
- One Groq batch run per verse per language at import time
- A new DB column or table to store results
- A schema migration

This is the same pattern as `word_insights` — batch AI generation at import time, stored permanently, served instantly. Want me to spec it out as a GitHub issue?

You're right, and the document does point that direction — NLLB is the open-source equivalent of what Google Translate uses under the hood. The user confirmed Google Translate produced smooth results from raw Greek, which means a proven NMT engine already handles Koine Greek well.

**The honest picture of what's available:**

| Option | Koine Greek support | Cost | Self-hosted |
|--------|-------------------|------|-------------|
| Google Cloud Translation API | Confirmed working | ~$20/1M chars — NT is ~$16 total | No |
| Meta NLLB-200 (HuggingFace) | Uncertain — targets modern languages | Free | Yes, needs GPU |
| DeepL | No Ancient Greek | — | No |

Google Cloud Translation API is the practical answer. The NT is ~800,000 characters. One batch run costs roughly $16 and never needs to run again — same economics as the Groq word insights batch.

**The architecture this enables:**

Call the API per verse at import time, get a full English rendering, then use **word alignment** to map each Greek token to its English equivalent in the translation. Store that as `contextual_gloss` on `verse_word`. The interlinear word-for-word structure is preserved but each gloss is now context-aware.

**The one open question I'm not certain about** is the word alignment step — mapping "before the unrighteous" back to ἐπί τῶν ἀδίκων automatically. That requires either an alignment tool or a structured API call. I'd want to test this before committing to the approach.

4. API Contract Limitations

Your `GET /passage/{bookId}/{chapter}` endpoint returns a rigid structure where fields like `liteGloss`, `englishGloss`, and `corpusGloss` map out standard dictionary outputs.

* **The Gap:** The UI has no way of telling the user, *"This word wasn't actually in the text; our AI reconstructed it with 84% confidence."*
* **The Fix:** Expand your `TokenDto`  JSON contract to include:


```json
"isReconstructed": true,
"reconstructionConfidence": "high",

```

## [assistant] (16:53:37)

This critique is valid, but the framing in the document is wrong for your use case. It says "this word wasn't in the text" — meaning corrupted/missing text. That's not your problem.

**The real version of this gap for an interlinear:**

Traditional printed interlinears have always dealt with this. When a translator adds an English word that has no Greek equivalent — for grammatical smoothness — they print it in *italics* or [brackets]. The KJV does this throughout. It's called a **supplied word**.

With the NMT/Google Translate approach, this becomes concrete:

- Greek articles (ὁ, τῶν) may map to nothing in English  
- English requires "does" for a question — no Greek word for it
- κρίνεσθαι (one Greek word) may map to "go to law" (three English words)
- Some Greek particles (μέν, δέ) have no clean English equivalent

When you align an NMT output back to individual Greek tokens, some tokens will have no aligned English word, and some English words will have no Greek source token. **That alignment gap is exactly what the `TokenDto` can't currently express.**

So the fix isn't `isReconstructed` — it's:

```json
"isSupplied": true,
"glossConfidence": "low"
```

Where `isSupplied` means "this English word was added for grammatical English, no Greek source token" — which the UI renders in italics, just like printed interlinears have done for centuries.

This is the right version of critique #4. Worth capturing in the design issue?

  "liteGloss": "law",
  "suppliedPrefix": "go to"
}
